# Decision log

The source of truth for settled questions, alongside `CALX_SPEC.md`. Add new decisions at the bottom with the next number. Never edit an old entry; supersede it with a new one that names it.

| # | Date | Decision | Choice | Applies from |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| D1 | 2026-10-26 | Target version | 26.3. Support one version at a time, and stay on 26.3 until Phase 5 is done | Phase 0 |
| D2 | 2026-10-26 | Scope | Server only, no client | Phase 0 |
| D3 | 2026-10-26 | Concurrency | Region actors only | Phase 0 |
| D4 | 2026-10-26 | Calcite | Written from scratch, with no code from other servers | Phase 0 |
| D5 | 2026-10-26 | Calx | Written from zero, with no Goose code. Goose's design notes may be read for ideas | Phase 0 |
| D6 | 2026-10-26 | Calx compiler | Written in C++, emitting C, built with clang | Phase 3 |
| D7 | 2026-10-26 | Platforms | Windows and Linux from the first commit; macOS later | Phase 3 |
| D8 | 2026-10-26 | License | MIT. Don't read GPL servers (Pumpkin, Paper, Folia) | Phase 0 |
| D9 | 2026-10-26 | Parity | Exact in single-region tests. Multi-region runs are deterministic and match single-region runs on everything not driven by randomness. Global state lives in one central world actor | Phase 5 |
| D10 | 2026-10-26 | Vanilla bugs | Functional parity: bugs and exploits are fixed, not copied, and each fix is listed in `rig/differences/` as a deliberate difference | Phase 1 |
| D11 | 2026-10-26 | Rig runner | Python | Phase 1 |
| D12 | 2026-10-26 | Proxy support | Velocity modern forwarding built in | Phase 4 |
| D13 | 2026-10-26 | Bedrock | Not built in. Left to a proxy (Geyser standalone) or a plugin (Floodgate-style auth) | — |
| D14 | 2026-10-26 | Plugins | Native Calx plugins first, WASM also supported, both built against one host API | Phase 0 |
| D15 | 2026-10-26 | Native plugin limits | Host API only by default. `extern` C calls only for plugins marked trusted in the server config | Phase 0 |
| D16 | 2026-10-26 | Plugin instances | One instance per region plus one global instance per plugin, talking by messages | Phase 0 |
| D17 | 2026-10-26 | WASM runtime | Embed Wasmtime (Apache 2.0). A custom WASM-to-C compiler is an option after Phase 8 | Phase 7 |
| D18 | 2026-10-26 | Goals and tie-breaks | Three goals: plugin development, performance, vanilla parity (bugs excepted). Parity wins wherever players, farms or data packs could observe the difference. Performance wins wherever nothing is observable. The plugin API never forces a parity or performance compromise in core | Phase 0 |
| D19 | 2026-10-26 | Tick model | Lockstep: every region finishes tick N before any region starts tick N+1. Regions are tasks on a work-stealing pool with more regions than threads. Why: it's the only model that keeps results identical across thread counts and gives world-wide operations vanilla's semantics. Cost: the heaviest region sets the tick time. Mitigated by splitting hot regions and moving parallel work (lighting, pathfinding, worldgen, packet encoding) off the region's critical path | Phase 0 |
| D20 | 2026-10-26 | Tick phases | Each tick runs a global phase (central world actor with exclusive access to the whole world), then the region phase (all regions in parallel), then message exchange (delivery ordered by sender region ID, then send sequence). Anything that can reach beyond one region runs in the global phase: unbounded selectors, data pack functions, sleeping, world-wide spawners, raids, world border, dragon fight. The exact order within a tick is settled in `CALX_SPEC.md` and judged by the rig | Phase 0 |
| D21 | 2026-10-26 | Determinism contract | The same tick-indexed input log gives the same output with any thread count. Inputs are packets (tagged with the tick that handles them), finished chunk loads and generations, console input, and results of plugin I/O. Calcite can record the log and replay it from Phase 4 on. This is what "one thread or many, same answer" means | Phase 0 |
| D22 | 2026-10-26 | Region layout | Region split and merge decisions are a pure function of world state at the tick barrier. Region IDs are assigned deterministically | Phase 5 |
| D23 | 2026-10-26 | Memory safety | Calx is memory-safe by default: bounds checks (elided when the compiler can prove them), no null (optional types instead), and no pointer arithmetic or unchecked casts outside `unsafe`. Only Calcite core and trusted plugins may use `unsafe` and `extern`. Why: without this, the plugin trust boundary (D15) is fake, because an untrusted plugin could corrupt the server without touching `extern` | Phase 0 |
| D24 | 2026-10-26 | References to game objects | Entities, players, chunks and block entities are referred to by generational handles checked on use, not pointers. A stale handle (the entity died or left the region) gives an error the caller must handle | Phase 0 |
| D25 | 2026-10-26 | Panics | A panic in a plugin instance stops that instance while its region keeps ticking. A plugin that keeps panicking is disabled. A panic in Calcite core writes a crash report, then saves and stops, the way vanilla does on a crash | Phase 0 |
| D26 | 2026-10-26 | Generics and modules | Calx has generics and interfaces from v0, monomorphized into C. Each module compiles to its own C translation unit, so builds are separate and incremental. The emitter adds `restrict` wherever ownership proves there's no aliasing, which closes Rust's main codegen advantage over C | Phase 0 |
| D27 | 2026-10-26 | Java-compatible collections | Calx's standard library includes Java-compatible `HashMap`, `HashSet` and `LinkedHashMap`, plus fastutil-compatible open hash collections. Calcite uses them wherever vanilla's iteration order is observable, for example the `HashSet<BlockPos>` behind locational redstone, and fast native collections everywhere else. Where vanilla iterates identity-hashed collections, its order isn't reproducible, so Calcite uses a fixed order and lists it as a deliberate difference | Phase 0 |
| D28 | 2026-10-26 | Strings | Calx strings are UTF-8 internally. The standard library provides Java's `String.hashCode` (over UTF-16 code units), UTF-16 length for protocol limits, and a Modified UTF-8 codec for NBT | Phase 0 |
| D29 | 2026-10-26 | Worldgen determinism | Worldgen output is a pure function of seed, data packs and chunk position, whatever the generation order or thread count. Features are decorated in a fixed order: chunks are colored by (x mod 3, z mod 3), and each color class is decorated in turn. Chunks in one class are 3 apart, so their 3×3 write areas never overlap. The rig drives vanilla in the same order. Vanilla's order- and path-dependent bugs (MC-55596, MC-272481, MC-241546) are listed as deliberate differences | Phase 5 |
| D30 | 2026-10-26 | Vanilla content | Neither repo nor any Calcite release contains Mojang content. The build and the first run download the official server jar from Mojang, checked against the SHA-1 in Mojang's version manifest, and Calcite reads the built-in data pack and structure templates from it. A local jar can be supplied instead | Phase 2 |
| D31 | 2026-10-26 | World format | Calcite reads and writes only its own version's world format and has no data fixers. Older worlds are upgraded by opening them in vanilla first. On a version bump, Calcite's worlds are upgraded the same way, which works because worlds round-trip | Phase 5 |
| D32 | 2026-10-26 | Native libraries | libdeflate (MIT) for zlib and deflate, lz4 (BSD-2) for vanilla's LZ4 region-file option, and one vendored crypto library, chosen in Phase 3, that covers AES with AES-NI, RSA PKCS#1 v1.5, SHA-1, SHA-256, HMAC-SHA256, MD5 and a TLS 1.3 client. AES-CFB8 is written directly on the AES block function | Phase 3 |
| D33 | 2026-10-26 | Networking | Event-driven I/O: IOCP on Windows, epoll on Linux. Connections live in a network layer that routes packets to the region owning each player. Packet encoding, compression and encryption run off the region threads, and regions never block on I/O | Phase 3 |
| D34 | 2026-10-26 | Performance tracking | Performance is tracked from Phase 3, not just measured in Phase 8. The tick-loop benchmark comes first, then a fixed bot scenario from Phase 4. Results go in the repo per commit, and a regression over 5% gets investigated before more work lands. CI runners are too noisy to gate on, so the numbers come from one fixed machine | Phase 3 |
| D35 | 2026-10-26 | Host API stability | The host API follows semver. A plugin built against major version N loads on any Calcite with the same major version, native or WASM. Breaking changes need a major bump and a deprecation cycle | Phase 7 |
| D36 | 2026-10-26 | Plugin data storage | Plugin persistent data lives in Calcite's own files next to the world, keyed by UUID, handle or position, so vanilla round trips never lose it. Data that must travel with an item or entity uses vanilla's `custom_data` component | Phase 7 |
| D37 | 2026-10-26 | Phase 3 gate failure | A mismatched RNG probe is a bug, and it gets fixed. If the benchmark is more than 10% slower than Rust, hand-write the same loop in C. If hand-written C is also slow, fix the benchmark or the compiler flags. If it's fast, the gap comes from Calx semantics, and the semantics change (for example, unchecked indexing in core modules) before any Calcite code is written. Rethinking means changing Calx, not replacing it | Phase 3 |
| D38 | 2026-10-26 | Repo layout | Two repos side by side: `calx` (language, compiler, runtime, standard library) and `calcite` (server, plugins, rig). Calcite's build finds the compiler at `../calx`, overridable with `CALX_HOME`, and from Phase 3 records the calx commit it was tested with | Phase 0 |
| D39 | 2026-10-26 | Line endings | LF everywhere via `.gitattributes`, so case-file hashes are the same on Windows and Linux | Phase 1 |
| D40 | 2026-10-26 | Docs as you go | Docs are written in the same change as the feature, never after. Calx docs live in `calx/docs/` as Markdown: one topic per page, front matter with `title` and `description`, relative links, and page order set in `docs/README.md`. That's enough for any common site generator (Starlight, VitePress, mdBook) to build the docs website later. Each feature lives in exactly one place: `CALX_SPEC.md` while it's designed but not built, then its page in `docs/reference/` once it ships, and that page becomes the normative text | Phase 0 |
| D41 | 2026-10-26 | Tested docs | From Phase 3, every code example in the Calx docs is extracted and compiled by the compiler test suite, and run where it shows output. An example meant to fail is marked with the error it should produce. From Phase 7, host API reference docs are generated from doc comments in the host API definition, and a function without one fails the build | Phase 3 |
| D42 | 2026-10-27 | Plugin state | Region code in a plugin has no instance state. State lives on players, chunks and entities (and moves with them), in the one global instance, or in the config, whose derived values are computed at load and shared read-only. Why: regions split and merge (D22), and per-region state would need plugin code to split and merge it. None of the five samples needed it | Phase 0 |
| D43 | 2026-10-27 | Views and handles | Live game objects (`Player`, `Region`, attached data) are views: second-class references that can't be stored, captured by a closure or sent, so plugin code never writes lifetimes and region scoping is checked at compile time. Handles (D24, such as `PlayerRef`) can be stored and sent, and resolve to a view only in the region where the object is. A closure that runs later may capture only sendable values and gets its object back as a fresh view | Phase 0 |
| D44 | 2026-10-27 | Plugin randomness and time | Plugins never draw from vanilla's random streams. Each plugin gets its own stream per region, seeded from the world seed, plugin ID and region ID, and one in the global instance. Wall-clock time is read once at the start of each tick and recorded in the input log (D21), and every handler in that tick sees the same value. Why: installing a plugin mustn't change what vanilla rolls, and replays must match | Phase 7 |
| D45 | 2026-10-27 | Filtering signed chat | Plugins can cancel a signed chat message or mask ranges of it with vanilla's filter mask, which keeps the signature valid. They can't change its text. A plugin that wants different text cancels the message and sends its own system message | Phase 7 |
| D46 | 2026-10-27 | Message delivery | A message sent in tick N is handled in tick N+1 (D20). To a player, it goes to their current region; if they're offline it's dropped, or kept and delivered when they join if the sender asks. To a position, it goes to whichever region owns that chunk at delivery; if the chunk isn't loaded, the sender chooses between loading it and delivering when it next loads. Kept messages are saved with the player's or chunk's plugin data | Phase 7 |
| D47 | 2026-10-27 | Region layout rule | Any two loaded chunks closer than the merge distance are in the same region, so a contiguous loaded area is one region and anything a region ticks can reach only its own chunks or unloaded ones. Whatever jumps across unloaded space (ender pearls, portals, teleports) is a handoff. The merge distance is set in Phase 5 | Phase 5 |
| D48 | 2026-10-27 | Calx memory model | Values are owned. Parameters are read-only views, mutable views (`mut`) or moved ownership (`own`). Views are second-class (D43), so there are no lifetimes, and the compiler rejects overlapping mutable views within a function. `checkout` takes one element out of a collection until the end of the block, keeping its place in the order, for the element-plus-owner case (an entity and its region) | Phase 0 |
| D49 | 2026-10-27 | Parallelism | Game state is made parallel only by `parallel for` over owned values: each iteration gets exclusive access to its element, and everything else it touches must be read-only for the whole loop, which the compiler checks. I/O and worker threads (network, chunk loading, lighting, worldgen) are Calcite core services that talk to the tick loop through queues, and their results enter as inputs (D21). Plugins never see threads | Phase 0 |
| D50 | 2026-10-27 | Tick phases, revised | Supersedes the phase order in D20 and the timing in D46. Each tick: cut the inputs, then the packet phase (regions in parallel: last tick's messages, then this tick's packets), then the global phase, then the world phase (regions in parallel: scheduled ticks, chunks, block events, entities, block entities, connections), then exchange at the barrier. A message is handled the next time its destination runs. Why: vanilla handles all packets before functions and world ticking, so this keeps a player's command seeing vanilla's game time and lets escalated commands run in the same tick | Phase 0 |
| D51 | 2026-10-27 | Commands across regions | A command runs in its region only if it provably touches nothing outside: `@s`, positions in the region's chunks, `@p`/`@n` when the nearest match is closer than any other region, and other selectors only with bounds inside the region. Otherwise it escalates to the global phase: the same tick for player commands, the next tick for command blocks. Functions are checked once per reload, and functions with macros escalate | Phase 4 |
| D52 | 2026-10-27 | Single-region exactness and lent state | Refines D9. Server-wide and world-wide state that regions use constantly (random sequences, unique seeds, entity IDs, scores, maps, the custom spawners) is lent: with one region it's owned by that region and behaves exactly like vanilla; with several it's split per region or changed by messages at the barrier. Multi-region runs are deterministic but can differ from single-region runs in timing and randomness, as listed in `docs/global-systems.md`. The rig compares single-region runs with vanilla and checks multi-region runs for determinism | Phase 5 |
| D53 | 2026-10-27 | Interaction radius | The merge distance (D47) is at least 9 chunks, so every bounded vanilla query (128 blocks at most: despawning, local mob caps, POI searches) is answered inside one region. Unbounded questions, such as whether a world has any player at all, are answered from the player directory | Phase 5 |
| D54 | 2026-10-27 | Real time in vanilla systems | Extends D44 to Calcite core. Anything in vanilla that reads real time (stopwatches, idle timeouts) reads the wall clock recorded at the start of the tick, so replays repeat it | Phase 4 |
| D55 | 2026-10-27 | Integer division by zero | Panics in Calx, for `/` and `%` on every integer type. `checked_div` returns an optional instead. Why: Java throws `ArithmeticException` and vanilla never catches it, so a panic gives the same outcome, and silently returning a value would hide bugs | Phase 3 |
| D56 | 2026-10-27 | Native hash maps | Calx's native hash maps have no per-process random seed, so the same operations always iterate in the same order. Code that leaks a native map's order by mistake is still deterministic across thread counts and replays (D21), even if it doesn't match vanilla | Phase 3 |
| D57 | 2026-10-27 | Attributes and prefixes | Attributes (`@packet`, `@command`, `@save`) and string prefixes (`t"..."`) are defined by libraries as Calx functions the compiler runs at compile time. They get a typed description of the declaration, return declarations as data, are deterministic, and can read only files their package lists as build inputs. Why: the host API and packet codegen shouldn't be hard-coded into the compiler | Phase 3 |
| D58 | 2026-10-27 | Transcendental functions | Calx's `std.math` ports fdlibm, so it matches Java's `StrictMath` on every platform. HotSpot's `Math` intrinsics, which vary by CPU, aren't ported, since HotSpot is GPL (D8's reasoning). The rig (`P1.5`) checks every function vanilla calls on the inputs it uses, and `Mth`'s sine table exhaustively. A difference players could see goes in `rig/differences/` unless a clean implementation from published algorithms can match it | Phase 1 |
| D59 | 2026-10-27 | Copying code | Copy code only when it's clearly the best option, for performance or exactness, and nothing better exists. Its license must allow it in an MIT project: MIT, BSD, Apache 2.0, fdlibm's notice and the like. Keep its license notice and record where it came from in the file. GPL code can't be copied, since it would make Calx and Calcite GPL (D8). Otherwise, write our own from algorithms, specs and vanilla's behaviour as the decompile and the rig show it | Phase 0 |
| D60 | 2026-10-27 | Floating-point reference and `std.math` | Supersedes D58's method. `std.math` ports fdlibm, with its notice kept (D59), so it gives `StrictMath`'s results bit for bit. The rig's reference is vanilla on HotSpot with `-XX:+UnlockDiagnosticVMOptions -XX:-UseLibmIntrinsic`, where `Math` matched `StrictMath` bit for bit on 22 million arguments (JDK 25, x86-64, Windows and Linux). With HotSpot's default x86-64 intrinsics, `Math` differs from `StrictMath` by 1 to 2 ULP on 1-10% of `sin`, `cos`, `tan`, `pow`, `log`, `log10`, `exp`, `cbrt` and `tanh` results. Matching that would mean copying HotSpot's GPL stubs, which D59 rules out. The rig records both configurations and reports where they differ, so the cost is measured | Phase 1 |
| D61 | 2026-10-27 | Compiled-in tables and startup data | Calcite compiles in what vanilla defines in code, from the converted data generator reports: block states, the built-in registries and their IDs, item default components, packet IDs, the command tree, and the code-defined biome parameter lists. Codegen makes them constant arrays, so a lookup is arithmetic and an array read with nothing parsed at startup. What vanilla's built-in data pack holds (data-driven registries such as biomes, dimension types and damage types, worldgen settings, tags, loot tables, recipes, advancements, structure templates) Calcite loads at startup from the jar, through the same loader user data packs use, so packs can override it as in vanilla. This reads D30's "Mojang content" as Mojang's code, assets and data pack files. The compiled-in tables are the identifiers, IDs and default values the protocol and save format need, which every compatible server has to encode. Why: the code-defined set is fixed per version, so compiling it in is fastest; the data pack set has to stay overridable | Phase 2 |
| D62 | 2026-10-27 | Pinned server jars | `oracle/versions.json` pins each game version's server jar: URL, size and SHA-1, taken from Mojang's version manifest (with the metadata's own SHA-1 checked) when the version is added. Builds check the local jar against the pin offline and download only when it's missing or wrong. Refines D30's check against the manifest: the manifest is read once, when pinning. Why: builds work offline, every checkout uses the same bytes, and a change upstream can't silently change the jar | Phase 2 |
| D63 | 2026-10-27 | Strict types | Calx's checker is stricter than Rust's, and every rule is an error with no flag to relax it (warnings exist only for deprecation). `T(x)` converts only when every value of `x`'s type is exactly a `T`. Lossy conversions say what they do: `T.wrap(x)` keeps the low bits (Java's narrowing casts), `i32.saturate(f)` and `i64.saturate(f)` are Java's float-to-integer casts, `T.nearest(x)` rounds to a float, and `T.try_from(x)` returns `T?`. `usize` is judged at both its widths. Optionals are built with `some(x)`, never implicitly. A literal must fit its type, hex included. An expression statement must be unit (discard with `_ = f()`). Unused locals, parameters, imports and private items are errors, as are a `var` never reassigned, a `mut` parameter never mutated, unreachable code and shadowing. `and` with `or`, mixed bitwise operators, shifts with `+` or `-`, and `??` with other operators need parentheses. A constant shift count must be under the type's width, and comparing with a NaN constant is an error. Why: Calcite ports Java, where silent conversions and precedence slips are where bit-exactness breaks; catching them at compile time is cheaper than finding them with the rig | Phase 3 |
| D64 | 2026-10-27 | Build speed | Building and running Calx code must be fast, unlike Rust. The front end (lexing, parsing, checking, emitting C) is written for throughput: arena allocation, interned names, no per-node heap work, and no whole-program analysis beyond what monomorphization needs. Each module is its own C file, and clang compiles only C whose text changed, in parallel, from a cache keyed by the C text, flags and compiler version. The runtime is compiled once per target and mode. Debug builds use `-O0` with line tables, and both platforms link with lld. `calx run` reuses the last executable when nothing changed. Build time is tracked like run time (D34), with a generated large program as its benchmark. When the two conflict, run time wins in release builds and build time wins in debug builds | Phase 3 |
| D65 | 2026-10-27 | Editor support | Starts in Phase 3 instead of waiting for the language server (P9.2). The VS Code extension in `calx/tools/vscode` has a TextMate grammar (also used for ```calx blocks in Markdown) and shows errors as you type by piping the editor's text to `calx check --json --stdin`, which prints one JSON diagnostic per line with 0-based lines and UTF-16 columns, as the Language Server Protocol counts them. That format is stable: the test suite checks it matches the text form on every error test. The language server replaces the extension's check runner and reuses the format. Why: `calx check` takes milliseconds, so checking on each change costs nothing, and seeing D63's errors while typing makes the strict checker pleasant to use | Phase 3 |
| D66 | 2026-10-27 | Enums | Calx enums are sum types: a variant holds nothing, values by position, or named fields, and `match` is the only way to read its data. Only an enum without data may name an integer representation (`enum Flag: u8 { OnGround = 1 }`), with values counting up from the previous one. It converts to a number as its representation would, exactly, and back with `Flag.try_from(n)`, which takes the representation's type and returns `Flag?`. Without one, the tag is the smallest of `u8`, `u16` and `u32` that fits and has no number a program can see. Data-less enums compare with `==` and print their variant's name; enums with data do neither until derived `Equal` and `Display` exist. Why: protocol and save-format enums need fixed values, and a tagged union laid out as C would lay it out costs nothing to match on | Phase 3 |
| D67 | 2026-10-27 | Optionals | `T?` is a `T` followed by a `bool`, with no allocation. Optionals don't nest: `T??` doesn't parse and `some(some(x))` doesn't check, so `?.` on an optional field gives that field's optional unchanged. `a ?? b` runs `b` only when `a` is `none`; with a `T?` on the right it gives a `T?`, so `??` chains. `==` compares two optionals element-wise, with IEEE rules inside. `opt!` panics on `none`. The `checked_` methods return `T?`: `MIN.checked_div(-1)` is `none`, since the quotient doesn't fit, but `MIN.checked_rem(-1)` is `some(0)`, which is what Java's `%` gives. Why: no nesting makes field chains and `??` simple, and nested optionals were never needed in the samples or sketches | Phase 3 |
| D68 | 2026-10-27 | Patterns and match | A `match` must cover every value and every arm must be able to match, checked with Maranget's usefulness algorithm, with integer ranges split into intervals (a `rune` excludes surrogates). The error shows a missing value. A guarded arm covers nothing. A capitalized name in a pattern is a variant or constant, never a binding, so a misspelled variant is an error instead of a catch-all as in Rust. Variant patterns may leave out the enum's name, since the input's type says which enum it is. Record patterns name every field or end in `..`. Floats can't be patterns. Patterns bind copies, so there's no `mut` binding. Or-patterns can't bind names yet. `let` takes a pattern that always matches, `let ... else` and `if let` one that might not, and on an optional both match the value inside. An arm body may be `return`, `break`, `continue` or an assignment. A `match` compiles to a chain of tests whose last arm is never tested. Why: exhaustiveness is how a new variant finds every place that must handle it, which matters when porting vanilla's large enums | Phase 3 |
| D69 | 2026-10-27 | Print evaluation order | `print`, `println`, `assert` and `panic` evaluate every interpolation, left to right, before writing anything, as Java builds the whole string before printing it. A function called inside `{...}` that prints therefore writes first. Why: printing piece by piece interleaved output differently from Java, which the rig would flag as a difference | Phase 3 |
| D70 | 2026-10-27 | Generics | Generic functions, structs and enums are checked once, where they're written, against their parameters' bounds only. Each use with real types makes a copy: the body is cloned and checked again with those types, so the emitter only ever sees ordinary code, and every generic call is a direct call clang can inline. A definition with errors has them reported once, and its copies aren't checked; an error that only some types have carries a note naming the use. Copies nest at most 64 deep, which catches a function calling itself with ever bigger types. Unused generic parameters, and impl parameters missing from the impl's type, are errors. This refines D67: through a parameter, optionals nest (`T?` with `T` being `i32?`), and `none` on the right of `??` on such a value is the inner optional, so `r ?? none` takes one level off. Written out, `i32??` still doesn't parse. C names list the types: `choose__i64`, `Pair_i32_str`. Why: Calcite's hot paths need generic code at hand-written speed, and checking at the definition reports an error once, where it is, instead of at every use as C++ templates do | Phase 3 |
| D71 | 2026-10-27 | Generic arguments and inference | In an expression, `<` right after a name with no space starts its generic arguments: `choose<i64>(...)`, `Pair<i32, str>.of(...)`. With a space it's less-than, and `f <T>(x)` gets an error saying to remove the space. A call works its parameters out from, in order: written arguments, the receiver, the arguments left to right with untyped literals (`1`, `none`) waiting for the others, and the expected result type. A parameter still unknown is an error, never a default. Why: the space rule avoids Rust's `::<>` while keeping `a < b` unambiguous, and letting literals wait means `choose(c, 1, 2i64)` picks `i64` instead of failing on `i32` | Phase 3 |
| D72 | 2026-10-27 | Interfaces | An interface holds methods, static functions, typed constants and default methods, with `Self` for the implementing type. An impl matches the interface exactly, parameter names included, since calls can name their arguments, and holds nothing else; its missing members are listed in one error. Because the interface fixes the names, an impl member's unused parameter isn't an error. Structs, enums and primitive types can implement interfaces, each at most once, and overlapping generic impls are errors. A private interface's impl for a private type that's never used is an error. A method is looked up in the type's own impls before its interfaces', and a name two interfaces share needs `Iface.method(x)`. An interface constant is a constant in every copy, so `T.sides` can be a field's default. Calls are direct and constants are inlined. Impl functions' C names include the interface, as in `Lift__Up__go`. `dyn`, associated types, generic interfaces, generic functions in interfaces, defaults for interface constants and the operator interfaces come later. Why: exact conformance is the strict choice (D63), and naming the interface in C keeps profiles readable | Phase 3 |
| D73 | 2026-10-27 | Modules and imports | A file is a module, and module `a.b.c` is `a/b/c.calx` under the source root. The root is the main file's folder, or, when the main file has a `module` line, the folder its parts lead back to. A file in a folder must start with its `module` line, matching its place, so any file checked alone, as editors do, finds the root; a file at the root can leave it out. Names match exactly, case included, on every system. Imports are `import a.b.{X, Y}` only: bare `import a.b` is an error until qualified access (`b.f()`) exists. An import that's never used is an error, and an imported name can't shadow one the module declares or already imported. Imports can't form a cycle, and the error lists the chain. A program is the main file and everything it imports, checked as a whole. Why: one place for each module and no cycles keep builds simple and fast (D64), and exact case stops a program that builds on Windows from failing on Linux | Phase 3 |
| D74 | 2026-10-27 | Visibility and where impls go | Items, struct fields and impl functions are private to their module unless `pub`. Enum variants and interface members are visible with their enum or interface. A struct literal in another module can leave out only fields with defaults, so a private field without one means the struct is built only by its module. A `pub` function's parameters and result, a `pub` struct's `pub` fields and a `pub` enum's variants can't name a type that isn't `pub`. Inherent impls go in the type's module; an interface's impl goes in the interface's module or the type's, and a primitive's in the interface's. A type has one function of each name: its own impls can't reuse a name, or default, its interfaces give it, as in Java, which refines D72's lookup order. A call through a bound is pinned to the bound's interface, so every copy of generic code calls the same function. Why: private-by-default and the orphan rule are the strict choices (D63), and a single meaning for each name means a generic copy can't quietly call a different function than the one checked | Phase 3 |
| D75 | 2026-10-27 | Separate compilation | Each module becomes one C file holding exactly what it uses: its functions, a `static` copy of each generic instance they reach, and only the types, constants and declarations those name. Functions are `static` unless another file calls them. Compound types get structural C names (`cx_opt_i32`, `cx_arr3_f64`), hashed when over 96 characters, so every file names them alike without coordination. Each file's object is cached under a hash of its own C text, so editing one module recompiles only the files whose text changed, in parallel, and the link template expands `{calx-inputs}` to any number of objects. The front end still checks the whole program each build. Duplicate instances across files may be folded by the linker in release builds later. Why: per-file text that changes only when its inputs do is what makes the object cache work for large programs (D64); a test edit of one module cut a rebuild from 104 ms to 35 ms | Phase 3 |
| D76 | 2026-10-27 | Ownership | Every value has one owner, and values that own memory (`String`, `List`, and anything holding one) are freed when the owner ends, with no garbage collector or reference counts. `Copy` is derived, not declared: a type is `Copy` when it owns no memory, and a generic `T` is `Copy` only with the bound `T: Copy`. Assigning, passing to `own`, returning and putting a value in a literal or list move it. Read and `mut` parameters, loop elements and pattern names that only look are views, which can't be moved, stored or returned, so no lifetimes are needed. A field of an owned local can move out on its own; the local can't be used whole until it's put back. A pattern takes its value apart when it moves or `var`-binds an owning name (a syntactic scan), and otherwise its names are views that borrow the value until they end. New values that are only looked at live until the end of their statement. A value that might have moved is zeroed where it moves, since freeing zeroed bits does nothing, so there are no drop flags; moves are plain byte copies. The pass runs after checking, only when there are no other errors, and walks each loop twice to reach a fixed point. Why: Rust's safety without its lifetime annotations, at zero run-time cost, which is the performance goal (D26); deriving `Copy` means a struct that gains a `String` is checked as owning everywhere at once | Phase 3 |
| D77 | 2026-10-27 | Strings | `String` is a pointer, a length and a capacity, where a capacity of 0 means it doesn't own its bytes, so a string literal used as a `String` costs nothing until it changes. An interpolating literal outside `print` and the others builds a `String` by sending the printing functions' output into it, so built and printed text share one code path, floats included. `String(s)` copies a `str` unless it's a literal, since a `str` may later view memory another value owns. Methods that only look at text (`push_str`, `contains`, `starts_with`, `ends_with`) take a `str` or a `String`. `==` compares bytes and needs both sides the same type; string literal patterns match a `str` or a `String`. Why: free literals matter for Calcite, whose packets and logs are full of constant text, and one formatting path keeps Java's float output exact everywhere | Phase 3 |
| D78 | 2026-10-27 | Lists | `List<T>` is a pointer, a length and a capacity, growing by doubling to at least 4, with every method compiled to inline C. An element can't be moved out by indexing; `remove`, `swap_remove` and `pop` take elements out. A `for` loop views each element, and the list can't change or move during it. `swap` works out both indexes before checking either, as `Collections.swap` does, and `insert` past the end panics with its own message. A list prints as Java's `ArrayList.toString` does. Java's 1.5x growth is left to the designed `java.ArrayList`, for code where capacity can be seen. Why: doubling is the faster default, and the loop rule is what stops a list growing under a loop that's reading it | Phase 3 |
| D79 | 2026-10-27 | Leak check | Debug builds compile the runtime with `CX_DEBUG`, which counts live allocations; a program that ends with any reports it and exits with status 102. Every debug test is therefore a leak test, and a double free shows up as a negative count. The memory tests were also run under AddressSanitizer and UBSan on Linux, through a `CALX_CC` wrapper. Why: the compiler promises that every value is freed exactly once, so a leak is a compiler bug, and the suite should catch it without a separate tool | Phase 3 |
| D80 | 2026-10-27 | Shorter `#line` | A function's first `#line` directive names its file, and later ones in the same file give only the line; once a directive names another file, every later one in that function names its file again, since emitted text can be reordered. Why: absolute paths were a third of the C (a 57,000-line test went from 10.2 MB to 7.0 MB of C, and emitting from 71 ms to 58 ms), with the same line tables (D64) | Phase 3 |
| D81 | 2026-10-27 | `unsafe` and pointers | `unsafe { }` is a block, usable as a value, where pointers, C functions and `List.set_len` can be used. A block that does none of these is an error, and so is one inside another, so every `unsafe` marks something unsafe. `*T` and `*const T` are only ever values of their own inside `unsafe`: any expression whose value is a pointer outside it is an error, and a pointer can't be in a struct field, list, array, tuple, optional or generic parameter, or a Calx function's parameter or result. `read` and `write` need `Copy` elements, since copying a value that owns memory would make two owners, and `write` needs `*T`. Why: D23 needs pointers kept to code that says it's unsafe; keeping them out of other types for now means no safe code can hold one, so the boundary is a block, not a module, and easy to audit. Pointer fields come with the core data structures that need them | Phase 3 |
| D82 | 2026-10-27 | C functions | `extern "C" fn` and `extern "C" { }` declare C functions by their C names. Their parameters and results are integers, floats, `bool` and pointers to them, parameters are plain values (no `mut` or `own`), and calls need `unsafe`. One C name has one signature across the program, or it's an error. In C, each file that calls one declares it as `cx_c_name` with an asm label naming the real symbol (`__USER_LABEL_PREFIX__` included), so a declaration never conflicts with a header or a builtin, and identical declarations in two files are the same C. An unused private one is an error, as any function is. Which modules may use `extern` waits for plugins (D15) | Phase 3 |
| D83 | 2026-10-27 | Standard library location | `std.x` is `std/x.calx` under the Calx home, the folder that holds `runtime/`, wherever the program is, and `std` can't name a program's own module. The home is found without clang, so `calx check` works on a program that imports `std` on a machine without clang. Module C prefixes that would collide, such as `a.b` and `a_b`, get a `_` added. Why: `std` written in Calx over a few runtime functions keeps the compiler small, and it's compiled and cached like any module, so it costs a build nothing after the first | Phase 3 |
| D84 | 2026-10-27 | Standard input and error | `std.io` has `read`, `read_exact`, `write` and `flush` over bytes. `write` shares `print`'s buffer, and the buffer is written out before `read` waits, so a request-and-answer program needs no flush. `eprint` and `eprintln` are built-ins like `print`, writing to unbuffered standard error after writing out what standard output has waiting, so a terminal shows both in order. Standard input is binary on Windows, like the output. Why: the rig's adapter speaks a binary protocol on standard output, so it needs exact bytes in and out, and somewhere else to log | Phase 3 |
| D85 | 2026-10-27 | Parsing numbers | `T.parse(text)` gives `T?` until errors as values exist. Integers follow `Integer.parseInt` (and `parseUnsignedInt` for unsigned types: no `-`, even on `0`), but with ASCII digits only, unlike Java, which also takes other scripts' digits; no protocol or file sends those. Floats follow `Double.parseDouble`'s grammar exactly, then round correctly with `strtod`, and `f32.parse` rounds once with `strtof`, as `Float.parseFloat` does, which a test checks with a value where rounding twice differs. `to_bits`, `from_bits` (raw, keeping NaN payloads), `rotate_left`, `rotate_right`, `byte_at` and `String.from_utf8` (strict UTF-8, taking the list's memory) came with them, for the RNG and JSON code in the rig adapter | Phase 3 |
| D86 | 2026-10-27 | Random sources | Calcite's random sources are `Copy` structs, dispatched through generics rather than virtual calls, with no atomics: `LegacyRandom` (Java's LCG, which is both `LegacyRandomSource` and `SingleThreadedRandomSource`, since they give the same numbers), `XoroshiroRandom`, the two positional factories, and `WorldgenRandom<T>` over either. `RandomSource` is the interface every source implements, `Bits` is `BitRandomSource`'s `next(bits)`, and `BitRandomSource`'s formulas are generic functions over `Bits`, so each source compiles to straight-line code. A bound vanilla rejects with `IllegalArgumentException` panics, since in vanilla it's a crash too; the rig adapter checks bounds first to report vanilla's rejection. Vanilla's float literals (`5.9604645E-8F`, and `1.110223E-16F` in the decompiled source, which the bytecode shows is the double 2^-53) were read from the bytecode with `javap`, not from the decompiler | Phase 3 |
| D87 | 2026-10-27 | Calcite's rig adapter | The adapter is Calcite code in `src/rig/`, built by `rig/calcite-adapter/launch.py` with `calx build --release` on every start, which takes under half a second cold and about 50 ms when nothing changed, so the binary is never stale and nothing is checked in. The build's output goes to standard error, since standard output carries only frames. `CALCITE_DEBUG=1` builds it in debug mode, where it checks that it frees everything (D79). Its JSON keeps numbers as their text, so echoing one loses nothing, and the Python stub is gone | Phase 3 |
| D88 | 2026-10-27 | Time, arguments and `unsafe fn` | Calx gets `unsafe fn`, a function only `unsafe` code can call, whose body is checked like any other. Interface functions, their implementations and `main` can't be one, since an interface's functions are callable from generic code that never wrote `unsafe`. `std.time.Instant.now()` is an `unsafe fn` over a monotonic clock (`QueryPerformanceCounter`, `CLOCK_MONOTONIC`), so the spec's "nothing in safe Calx can see time" holds: Calcite reads the clock once per tick and records it (D44, D54), and a benchmark reads it to time itself. `std.env.args()` is safe, since arguments are inputs: every argument is a valid UTF-8 `String`, from `GetCommandLineW` split by the C runtime's documented rules on Windows, and with each byte that can't start a UTF-8 sequence turned into U+FFFD on Linux. The `calx` driver carries a manifest that makes its Windows code page UTF-8, so its own arguments and paths aren't lossy either. Why: the benchmark needs both, and routing time through `unsafe` keeps the determinism rule enforceable rather than a convention | Phase 3 |
| D89 | 2026-10-27 | Faster than Rust | The target is Calx faster than Rust in every benchmark configuration, not within 10% of it: the 1.10 × gate in docs/benchmarks.md stays as the floor. The speed comes from the compiler, so every Calx program gains, and the benchmark keeps running the same algorithms as the Rust reference. Three compiler changes so far. Release builds use clang `-O3`, which is the level rustc's `--release` uses (`-O2` held Calx a level below), and the one that specializes a loop for each value of a test that's the same on every pass, such as a palette's layout. Every function starts with `CX_VIEW` for each view parameter, assuming it dereferenceable and aligned, which views guarantee for the whole call; clang then hoists a view's field reads out of loops, even after inlining, where Rust's parameter attributes are lost. And a `mut` argument conflicts only with arguments that reach the same memory: separate fields are separate values, as Rust allows. On one worker the tick went from 25.8 ms (level with Rust's 26.6 on the same day) to 17.9 ms. Why: performance is one of Calcite's three goals, and a language built for it should win, not tie | Phase 3 |
| D90 | 2026-10-27 | `parallel for` | Built over ranges, `parallel for k in a..b`, rather than the spec's `parallel for x in mut xs` first, since indexing with the loop's variable covers one list, several lists side by side (a region's blocks and its state), and nested grids alike. The capture check works on places as paths from a local, like D48's: an iteration may change a place only through `[k]` with the loop's own variable, reached through fields and indexes that are the same in every iteration, and may read only places no other iteration can change, where a list's length counts apart from its elements. `break` and `return` can't leave the body; `continue` can. The body becomes its own C function over a chunk of the range, taking the outer locals through a context struct (numbers by value, the rest by `restrict` pointer with `CX_VIEW`). The runtime's pool has persistent threads that claim chunks in order from a shared counter (up to 8 per thread), spin for 200 µs after a loop and then sleep on `WaitOnAddress` or a futex, and never wait for a sleeping thread. Output from the body is held per chunk and written in iteration order when the loop ends, a panic reports the first iteration that panicked after the output of every earlier one, and reading standard input inside panics, so a program behaves the same with one thread and with many, not only computes the same. Nested loops run on the thread that reaches them. `std.pool.set_workers(n)` sets the thread count, logical processors by default; the module isn't `std.parallel`, since `parallel` is a keyword. On the tick benchmark at 24 workers, Calx runs 2.3 ms per tick against Rust's 3.5 on Windows and 2.5 against 3.7 on Linux | Phase 3 |
| D91 | 2026-10-27 | Error values | `-> T ! E` and `fn f() ! E` declare a function that can fail. `return v` gives back the value, `fail e` returns the error, and a function that returns nothing succeeds at its end. A call's result, `T ! E`, is an instance of a built-in enum whose variants are `ok(T)` and `err(E)` (`ok` holds nothing when `T` is `()`), so patterns, exhaustiveness, ownership and the C layout are an enum's: a one-byte tag and a union, which fits in registers when small. A result is handled where the call is, by `try`, `match`, `if let`, `let ... else` or `!`. It can't be stored, passed, returned or discarded with `_ =`, so an error is never dropped without the code saying so, and no later code can forget to check one. That's stricter than Rust (D63), and it means a result never needs a drop of its own beyond the arm that takes it. `try` needs the call's error type to be the function's exactly until `From` conversions exist. `ok(...)` and `err(...)` aren't values, so there's one way to return each. `try` and `fail` can't be used in a `parallel for` body, as `return` can't. `try` can sit anywhere in an expression: on its error path it drops what a `return` there would, and an owned value worked out earlier in the same expression waits in a statement temporary that's zeroed as it moves on, so it's dropped on the way out and never twice. That also fixed the same leak for a `return` inside a `match` arm in an argument list. A C function can't fail, and an error type can't be `()` | Phase 3 |
| D92 | 2026-10-27 | std.net | TCP is completion-style events on IOCP (Windows) and edge-triggered epoll (Linux): a `Net` reports `Accepted`, `Connected`, `Received` and `Closed`, and nothing but `poll` waits. The runtime owns every buffer: bytes that arrived wait in the connection until `read` copies them out, and `write` appends to a queue the socket drains, so a program never lends memory to the system and safe code can't race a pending operation. A connection gets at most one `Received` per poll. A `Conn` is its slot and an 8-bit generation, so an old one never names a new connection, and a slot is reused only after the poll that follows its `Closed`, so what arrived before the close can still be read. `Net` and `Conn` are `Copy` handles, and a `Net` lasts until the program ends: freeing one waits for destructors, so no copy can outlive it. A `Net` panics inside a `parallel for`, and Calcite runs it on its own thread (D33, D49). Failing to listen or to open a socket is a `NetError` (D91); a connection that can't be opened is a `Closed` event, since knowing takes a round trip. A host with a character no name or address has fails at once, without asking a name server. The sockets are linked only into programs that use them. On loopback, 16 clients each echo 200,000 bytes on both platforms | Phase 3 |
| D93 | 2026-10-27 | Comparing and printing | `Equal` and `Display` are derived properties like `Copy`: every struct, enum, tuple, array, optional and list has them when everything it holds does, with no `derive` to write, so only a generic parameter without the bound lacks them, and a pointer can't be shown. `==` compares every field in order, private ones too, and floats inside compare as floats, so `NaN` never equals itself. `{x}` writes a value the way code builds it: `Point { x: 1, y: 2 }`, `Circle(1.5)`, `Rect { w: 2.0, h: 3.0 }`, `(1, a)`, `[1, 2]`, `some(5)` and `none`, with text unquoted as Java's lists show it. It shows private fields, so it's for logs and debugging until a type can write its own. `T: Equal` and `T: Display` are bounds like `T: Copy`, and the names are reserved. Each file gets a `static inline` `cx_eq_` or `cx_show_` function only for the types it compares or prints, and those they hold, so comparing two small structs is a few compares once inlined and showing one builds no strings. `fn main() ! E` writes `error: ` and the error to standard error, frees it and exits with status 1. Its `E` must have `Display`. `calx check` now checks a `main` that's there, without asking a library for one | Phase 3 |
| D94 | 2026-10-27 | Map and Set | `Map<K, V>` and `Set<T>` are built into the compiler like `List`, so each is specialized C for its key and value types: inline lookups and inserts with no hashing or comparing through pointers. The table is a SwissTable (`hashbrown`'s and Abseil's layout): one allocation of slots and control bytes, 16-byte groups matched with one SSE2 compare and a scalar fallback with the same layout, power-of-two sizes from 4, at most 7/8 full, and deleted bytes only where a lookup could have passed the slot. `put` finds the key and the first free slot in one probe; every walk over a table goes a group at a time. Keys need `Hash`, a third derived property: integers, `bool`, `char`, `rune` and strings have it, and so does what holds only those; floats, pointers, maps and sets don't, and `Hash` implies `Equal`. The hash is a folded multiply with a fixed seed (D56: no per-process randomness, so iteration order is the same everywhere), and every bit of it depends on every bit of the key, so packed positions that differ only in their high bits spread out, where Fx would pile them up. `m[k]` reads or changes a key that's there and panics otherwise; only `insert` adds. `String` keys are looked up as `str`, so lookups never allocate. `get` exists only for `Copy` values. `==` ignores order, and printing shows `{k: v}` in loop order. Measured on 10k random `i64` keys against Rust's `HashMap` in fresh processes: with the same hash, Calx is 13% faster on hits, 19% on misses, and level on removes and inserts; against Rust's default SipHash it's 1.6 to 3.6 times faster; against Fx, faster on hits and misses and 6 to 9% slower on removes and reserved inserts, the one cycle `mulx` costs over `imul` for the stronger hash. Growing tables in both languages is dominated by page faults from the system heap giving freed pages back, which is the allocator's to fix | Phase 3 |
| D95 | 2026-10-27 | Allocator | Release builds use Calx's own allocator, `runtime/calx_alloc.c`, instead of `malloc`. Every free gives the block's size, `cx_free(p, size)`, since the compiler always knows it (a list's `cap`, a `String`'s `cap`, a table's mask), so blocks have no header and a free never looks up what an address belongs to. Blocks of up to 32 KiB come in 40 size classes, 16-byte steps to 128 and then four per doubling, from per-thread free lists with no lock; a list moves whole, in O(1), to a shared one when a thread has freed 256 KiB more than it took, and a thread that runs out takes the shared list first, so memory flows from the threads that free to the threads that allocate. New small blocks are cut from 1 MiB chunks, and a thread that ends gives its lists and the rest of its chunk back. Bigger blocks come from `VirtualAlloc` or `mmap`, rounded to four sizes per doubling, and a freed one is cached, up to 256 MiB in all, for the next of its size. Why: the Windows heap gives big freed blocks back to the OS, so a table that grew and was freed faulted on every page of the next one (136 faults per round of the map benchmark, for Rust as much as Calx); mimalloc (MIT) was the first choice, but running code built from downloaded source was blocked, and a sized allocator is simpler than mimalloc because Calx always knows the size. Measured: growing a 10k-key map went from 0.175 to 0.091 ms (Rust: 0.172 to 0.187), and a million-key map now beats Rust with Fx on every operation, inserts by 1.8 times. The tick benchmark is unchanged or slightly faster. Debug builds keep `malloc`, with each block's size in a header: a free with the wrong size or a second free stops the program with status 102, so every debug test checks the compiler's sizes; AddressSanitizer builds and `CX_SYSTEM_ALLOC` use `malloc` too | Phase 3 |
| D96 | 2026-10-27 | Order and sorting | `Order` is a derived property like `Equal`: every struct, enum, tuple, array, optional and list has it when everything it holds does, and integers, `char`, `rune`, `bool` and strings have it. It gives `<`, `<=`, `>`, `>=`, `a.cmp(b)`, which returns the built-in enum `Ordering` (`Less`, `Equal`, `Greater`), and `xs.sort()`, and `T: Order` is a bound that gives them, and `==`, to generic code. Structs compare their fields in declaration order, enums their variants in declaration order whatever their values (Java's `ordinal`), lists element by element with the shorter first, optionals with `none` first (Java's `Comparator.nullsFirst`), and strings byte by byte, which for UTF-8 is code point order; Java's `String.compareTo` compares UTF-16 units, which differs only between characters above U+FFFF and those from U+E000 to U+FFFF. Floats, pointers, maps and sets have no `Order`: `<` still works on two floats, but NaN makes it no order at all, so a type holding a float can't be compared or sorted, and `x.total_cmp(y)` gives Java's `Double.compare` order instead. Optionals gained `<`, which D67 had left out, since a struct holding one has to be orderable. `sort` may use an unstable algorithm: a derived `Order` compares everything a value holds, so equal elements are the same value and stability can't be observed. When types can write their own `Order`, theirs will be sorted stably, as Java's `List.sort` is. The sort is `runtime/calx_sort.h`, a C template: a list in order or in reverse is found in one pass; integers in lists of 128 or more are radix sorted by the bytes that differ, skipping the rest, and rewritten in place by counting when only one byte differs; everything else is quicksorted with a branchless partition, median-of-3 or ninther pivots, equal pivots set aside in one pass, insertion sort (or a sorting network, for integers) below 17 and heapsort after too many bad splits. A struct or tuple of scalars compares with bitwise ands and ors and no branch. The partition copies elements memory to memory rather than from the fields it compared, as storing the fields one by one and loading the element whole stalled store forwarding and cost 45% on a struct sort. Integer sorts are compiled once into the runtime file `calx_sort.c`, linked only into programs that call them, as optimizing them takes clang a second; other types get a static instance in the file that sorts them. Why: against Rust 1.91 on the reference machine, Calx's `sort` beats Rust's `sort` in every case measured and `sort_unstable` in all but one: 1M random `i64` in 8.6 ms against 15.7 and 11.0, 1M `i32` in 3.9 against 13.1 and 10.6, 1M of 16 values in 0.89 against 6.0 and 3.0, 200k structs in 4.7 against 7.9 and 6.4, 200k strings in 7.7 against 15.0 and 13.0. 10k random `i64` take 0.066 ms against 0.075 and 0.053; wider radix digits and a merging leaf sort were tried there and were slower | Phase 3 |
| D97 | 2026-10-27 | Math functions | Two modules with the same functions, as D27 has two families of collections. `std.java.math` is the fdlibm port: `StrictMath`'s results bit for bit (D60), for code that must compute what vanilla computes; Calcite's ports of vanilla code import it. `std.math` is for everything else: within about half an ULP, Java's special values, and the same bits on every machine, since it's all Calx and uses no system library. Its `exp`, `log` and `pow` are its own: Tang's table-driven method with 128-entry tables and minimax polynomials that `tools/math_tables.py` generates at 80 digits. `log`'s 1/c values are multiples of 2^-7 or 2^-8, so z/c - 1 is exact in one fused multiply-add, and the two next to 1 are 1, so there's no separate path near 1. `pow` carries log(x) as two doubles to about 2^-68. Its other functions call `std.java.math` until a faster algorithm replaces one, without promising Java's bits. Floats get `x.mul_add(a, b)`, IEEE's fused multiply-add, which these rely on; `a * b + c` still never contracts (D63). Why: fdlibm isn't correctly rounded, so any faster algorithm differs from it in the last bit on some arguments, and one function can't be both. Parity matters only where vanilla's results can be seen, and speed everywhere else. On 1.6 million arguments checked against 45-digit values, the worst errors are 0.5035, 0.5054 and 0.5044 ULP and 99.9% are correctly rounded, so they're also more accurate than fdlibm. On the reference machine `exp`, `log` and `pow` take 1.27, 1.93 and 5.94 ns on Windows, against 2.46, 2.33 and 6.30 for the Windows CRT, and 1.29, 1.91 and 5.70 on Linux, against 2.23, 2.05 and 6.56 for glibc 2.43, which Rust calls. fdlibm's `pow` takes 29.5 | Phase 3 |
| D98 | 2026-10-27 | Renaming imports | `import a.b.{X as Y}` brings `X` in as `Y`, as the spec designed. `as` is a word only in an import list, so it stays free as a name. An item can be imported once, under one name, and `X as X` is an error, since each would only be a second way to say the same thing. Why: two modules can use one name, as `std.math` and `std.java.math` do, and a module that wraps another needs both; a wrapper of one call compiles to one jump | Phase 3 |
| D99 | 2026-10-27 | Whole-program optimization | Release builds compile each module's C to LLVM bitcode with `-flto=thin`, and lld optimizes and inlines across files when it links (ThinLTO), at `-O3` there too, which lld-link needs told. Each module is its own C file for parallel, cached compiles (D75), so until now a call from one module to another was never inlined, however small the function: a loop of `Vec3` methods from another module took 1338 ms without ThinLTO and 290 ms with it, and a loop packing block positions 152 ms and 20 ms. The runtime's files take part too. lld keeps each file's machine code in the cache's `lto` folder, at most 512 MB, so an edit compiles again only the files it could change: a release build of the 64-module build benchmark takes 0.89 s from an empty cache against 0.77 s before, and 0.26 s after an edit to one module against 0.24 s. Debug builds don't change. Without lld, each file is optimized alone. Rust's release profile does ThinLTO only within each crate, and inlines across crates only `#[inline]`, generic and very small functions; Calx optimizes the whole program by default. Since clang now sees that a call into another module has no side effects, it can move a loop past a reading of the clock, so `std.hint.black_box<T>(x: own T) -> T` hides a value from it, as Rust's `std::hint::black_box` does: its Calx body is `return x`, and the emitter puts before it an empty `asm volatile` that takes the argument's address and clobbers memory. | Phase 3 |
| D100 | 2026-10-27 | sin, cos and tan | `std.math`'s `sin`, `cos` and `tan` are its own, as D97 allows, since the Windows CRT beat fdlibm there (7.41 against 8.15 ns for `sin`). For x of magnitude under 2^20, x = k pi/2 + r with pi/2 as three doubles: x - k pio2_1 is exact in one fused multiply-add, and r comes as two doubles. The result is sin(r), cos(r), -sin(r) or -cos(r) as k mod 4 says; each is a + a r^2 c + a r^4 Q(r^2), with a = r or 1, so one sum computes all four and a table indexed by k mod 4 gives what differs. fdlibm branches on k, which random arguments mispredict three times in four. The first terms are summed as two doubles, so the last rounding is the only large one. `tan` is the quotient of two of these, corrected by its remainder. Polynomials are degree 5 in r^2, from `tools/math_tables.py`. Measured on 718,000 arguments against 50-digit values: sin within 0.533 ULP, cos 0.533, tan 0.553, 99.7-99.8% correctly rounded, where fdlibm is 0.80-0.81 ULP and 97-98%. Windows, ns per call: sin 4.34, cos 4.42, tan 8.11, against the CRT's 7.22, 7.06, 8.47; Linux: 4.19, 4.22, 7.98 against glibc's 11.68, 10.10, 12.04. Larger arguments, infinity and NaN call `std.java.math`. Both math benchmarks now keep each loop's sum from being dropped (`std.hint.black_box`, and an empty `asm` in C), which clang had done for 6 of the 7 runs; that raised exp's time from 1.27 to 1.38 ns, and the CRT's from 2.46 to 2.58. | Phase 3 |
| D101 | 2026-10-27 | Rounding, and no java_string | Java's `Math.round` is a conversion, written like the others: `i64.round(d)` takes an `f64` and `i32.round(f)` an `f32`, as `Math.round(double)` gives a `long` and `Math.round(float)` an `int`, and every other pairing is an error that names the right one. It's floor(x) + 1 when x - floor(x) >= 0.5, then the clamping cast, so 0.49999999999999994 rounds to 0, as it has since Java 7. `x.rint()` is Java's `Math.rint`, halves to even, in one instruction. The spec's `f.java_string()` isn't built: `"{x}"` already gives `Double.toString`'s text as a `String`, and a second way to say the same thing is what D98 refused for `X as X`. `saturate` and `round` can't give a constant's value, since they're calls in C; before, `const A: i64 = i64.saturate(2.5)` passed the checker and crashed the emitter. | Phase 3 |
| D102 | 2026-10-27 | Writing Equal, Display, Hash and Order | A struct or an enum writes its own derived property by implementing an interface of that name, which every module sees without an import: `fn eq(self, other: Self) -> bool`, `fn show(self)`, `fn hash(self, h: mut Hasher)` and `fn cmp(self, other: Self) -> Ordering`. They live in `std.core`, a module the compiler holds and every program has; their names and `Hasher` stay reserved, so no module can declare them, and as bounds they still mean the property, however a type has it. The derived glue of every type that holds one calls the written function, so a list of block positions shows each as `1, 64, -3`, and the functions are called only by what they're behind: `a.eq(b)`, `p.show()` and `Equal.eq(a, b)` are errors, so there's one way to compare or show a value whatever its type. `show` prints with `print`, which writes where the value is going (standard output, standard error, or the String an interpolation builds), so showing a value builds no string; `eprint` inside it now saves and restores where output was going. `hash` adds values with `h.add(x)` to a `Hasher` that nothing else can make, which mixes them as a derived Hash does, rather than returning a number the type mixes itself, as Java's `hashCode` does, where a poor mix is easy. A type with its own Equal has no derived Hash, which would hash what eq ignores and lose lookups; it writes Hash too to be a key, and Hash needs Equal. Its own Order may sit beside a derived Equal, as in Java and Rust, and a type with its own Order, or holding one, is sorted stably, as Java's `List.sort` is, since two different values can then compare equal and vanilla's comparator sorts rely on stability. The stable sort is a quicksort whose partition keeps order (left elements in place, the rest through a buffer, by pointer with no branch), with runs of 32 or fewer sorted by a stable 4-element network and merges from both ends; it beats Rust's stable sort_by everywhere measured: 22.1 against 27.0 ms for 1,000,000 random structs, 0.098 against 0.124 for 10,000, 6.6 against 10.8 for 16 distinct keys. A merge from both ends stays in bounds unless cmp contradicts itself, which a check after each merge finds, and the sort then panics rather than leave an element twice and another gone. Only structs and enums write their own; numbers, strings, lists and the rest keep theirs, and a generic type's impl gives an instance for each argument that meets its bounds. | Phase 3 |
| D103 | 2026-10-27 | Generic interfaces, and `From` for `try` | An interface can have parameters of its own after `Self`, as `interface Into<T>` does. Each use names its arguments, and each list of arguments is a separate interface, so a type implements `Into<i64>` and `Into<String>` both; in the checker each one is an item of its own, made once, so comparing two is comparing pointers. A bound names the arguments and can mention other parameters (`B: From<A>`), an impl for a generic type can use its parameters in them (`impl<A> Into<List<A>> for Pair<A>`), and defaults can use them. When a type implements one generic interface more than once, a call chooses the impl the way a generic call works out its types, from the arguments and then from where the result goes, as Java chooses between overloads and Rust infers a trait's parameters; a call that neither settles is an error. The checker chooses, so the C is a direct call, and each impl's C name carries the arguments, as `Meters__Into_i64__into`. `From<T>`, with `fn from(value: own T) -> Self`, is built into `std.core` beside `Equal`, and its name is reserved. `try` converts the call's error to the function's through it when their types differ, so a function that calls code with several error types fails with one enum of its own and passes each up with a plain `try`, as Phase 4's packet decoders need; a missing conversion is an error that says which `From` to implement. The error moves into `from`, so one that owns memory isn't copied, and in release a `from` that wraps the error in a variant is inlined. A type has no `From` of itself, unlike Rust's blanket impl, and writing one is an error, since `try` passes an error of the function's own type up as it is. | Phase 3 |
| D104 | 2026-10-27 | Java's view of a string, and Modified UTF-8 | `s.java_hash()` is Java's `String.hashCode` and `s.utf16_len()` its `length()`, worked out from the UTF-8 over the UTF-16 units it stands for, with nothing converted: `java_hash` takes eight ASCII bytes a step, `h * 31^8` plus each byte times its power, so the chain of multiplies is an eighth of Java's, and `utf16_len` counts the bytes that start a rune plus those that start a four-byte one, which clang vectorizes. `for r in s.runes()` goes over the runes and `for c in s.chars()` over the UTF-16 units, as Java's `charAt` does, decoded as the loop goes. They're only what a `for` loop goes over, not values, since nothing would hold them, and `for c in s` is an error that names both, so a loop always says which it means. `std.text.mutf8` is written in Calx over `chars()`: `encode` appends to a list, `encoded_len` measures, and `decode` reads between two offsets, so NBT reads a string where it sits; the length prefix is left to NBT and the protocol, which each write their own. `decode` accepts exactly what Java's `readUTF` does, a plain zero byte and longer forms included, and a surrogate without its pair, which Java holds but UTF-8 can't, becomes `?`, which is what Java writes for one in UTF-8, as the protocol does. Checked against Java 25: the hash, the length and the encoding on 20,000 random strings, and decoding on 30,000 random byte strings, every result the same. | Phase 3 |
| D105 | 2026-10-27 | JavaHashMap, JavaHashSet and JavaHash | `JavaHashMap<K, V>` and `JavaHashSet<T>` are Java's `HashMap` and `HashSet`, built into the compiler as a second kind of `Map` and `Set`, so indexing, loops, `==`, printing, copying, moving and freeing are theirs, and only the table's C differs. They're named so, not `java.HashMap`, since built-in types live in no module, and the name says what they are where Calcite uses them. After the same operations they iterate in Java's order, which comes from rules the tables keep, not from Java's code: the spread hash picks one of a power of two bins, 16 to start with; a key goes at the end of its bin, or the front for `compute_if_absent`, as `computeIfAbsent`, `compute` and `merge` add one; the table doubles past three quarters full, each bin splitting in order; and a bin of more than 8 in a table of 64 or more becomes a red-black tree, with Cormen et al.'s balancing, whose root goes first and whose new keys go after their parents. Trees order equal hashes by `Order` where the key has it, strings by UTF-16 unit as `compareTo` does, and otherwise in a fixed order where Java's depends on addresses. `JavaHashMap(n)` is `new HashMap<>(n)`, `reserve(n)` is the room `putAll` and the copy constructors make, `clear` keeps the table, and `copy()` is the same table, so Java's `clone` and copy constructors are written as `reserve` and inserts. `JavaHash`, `fn java_hash(self) -> i32`, is a fifth property: built into the integers, `bool`, `char`, `rune`, text and lists as Java's boxes, `String` and `List` hash, and written by a struct or an enum, which has no derived one, as a Java class writes `hashCode`. It needs `Equal`, a key needs it, and floats have `java_hash()` but not the property, since their `==` isn't `equals`. The table is an array of bins holding node indices, negative for a tree, and one array of nodes, each the spread hash, the next index and the key or entry in place, with tree links beside them only once a tree exists; lookups and inserts are inline C per key type, and growing and the trees are `calx_jtable.c`, linked only when used. Checked against Java 25 with the same random operations on both, over integer, `Long`, string and struct keys built to share bins and make trees: 60 runs, 1.92 million operations, the same order at all 240,060 checks. | Phase 3 |
| D106 | 2026-10-27 | What a `for` loop keeps from changing | A `for` loop keeps what it goes over from changing or moving until it ends, with what holds it and what's in it, and nothing else: in a loop over `r.scheduled` the body can change `r.due`, and not `r.scheduled` or `r`. A place is its variable and the fields to it, up to the first index, since two indexes can be the same: a loop over `rs[i].scheduled` keeps all of `rs`. Fields are storage of their own, never reached through a pointer in safe code, so a change to one can't reach another, and the rule is Rust's for fields, where Calx's had kept the whole variable. It came up when the tick benchmark took D105's `JavaHashMap` and collected the due ticks from one field of a region into another, which the old rule refused for no reason the program could see. The checker's changes and the ownership pass's moves both follow it, and their errors name the field the loop goes over. The benchmark gave the same checksum, as fast as its hand-written map: 17.8 ms a tick on 1 worker and 2.28 ms on 24, against 18.1 and 2.25 recorded. | Phase 3 |
| D107 | 2026-10-27 | OpenHashMap and OpenHashSet: fastutil's tables | `OpenHashMap<K, V>` and `OpenHashSet<T>` are fastutil's open hash maps and sets, which vanilla keeps most of its tables in, built in as a third kind of `Map` and `Set` beside Calx's own and Java's, so they share everything but their C. The key type picks the class, since the value type never changes the order: integer keys are the `Int2…` and `Long2…` classes, placed by `HashCommon.mix` of the number, with 0 marking an empty slot and the key 0 kept apart and first in a loop, and any other key is an `Object2…` class, placed by `mix` of its `JavaHash`. The order comes from rules fastutil 8.5.18 keeps, not from its code: linear probing from the hash, removal that shifts the rest of a run back, a rehash from the last slot down to the size `arraySize` gives when an insert finds the table at `maxFill`, a halving when removing leaves it a quarter full, loops from the last slot to the first, `putAll`'s presizing as `reserve`, `trim`, and the constructor's expected size and `float` load factor, worked out in `double` or `float` where fastutil does. A slot holds the key and value together, where fastutil has two arrays, so a hit reads one place, and a table of other keys keeps 30 bits of each key's mixed hash beside it, so finding compares words first and rehashing never hashes a key again. `Reference2…` tables, whose order is their keys' addresses, hash strategies, the linked tables and removal through an iterator are still to build. Checked against fastutil with the same random operations on both, over `int` keys with 0 and dense runs, `long`, string and record keys sharing hashes, random sizes and load factors from 0.25 to 0.99, and phases of adding and removing that grew tables to 4,096 slots and shrank them about 450 times a run: 60 runs, 3 million operations, the same order at all 375,060 checks. | Phase 3 |
| D108 | 2026-10-27 | What a string's parts hold until it's made | A string's `{...}` parts run left to right before anything is written, as Java builds the whole string first, and a part that's a variable, a field or an element is held as it is until then. A `Copy` value is copied when its part runs, so it shows that value. One that owns memory, such as a list or a map, isn't, so a later part that changes it or moves it, or what holds it or what's in it, is a compile error, with the same rule for fields and indexes as a `for` loop's (D106). Before, `{xs} {grow(mut xs)}` showed `xs` from memory `grow` could have freed, and `{m} {m.remove(k)}` showed the map after the removal. Rust rejects the same code, since its formatting borrows each argument; a part that only reads from the value, such as `{xs.len()}`, holds nothing, as there. | Phase 3 |
| D109 | 2026-10-27 | LinkedMap and LinkedSet: the order keys went in | `LinkedMap<K, V>` and `LinkedSet<T>` go over their keys in the order they went in, which is Java's `LinkedHashMap` and `LinkedHashSet` and fastutil's linked tables, such as the `Long2ObjectLinkedOpenHashMap` vanilla keeps its chunk holders in. No hashing changes that order, so they're one type for all those classes and every key type: Calx's own table (D94), whose keys need `Hash`, with a list through its full slots, two `cx_u32` links a slot in an array beside it, so finding a key costs what it costs in a `Map`. A new key goes last and one that's there keeps its place; `insert_first` and `insert_last` put a key at an end, moving it if it's there, as `putFirst` and `putAndMoveToFirst` do; `move_to_first` and `move_to_last` move one, as `getAndMoveToFirst` does; `pop_first` and `pop_last` take one out with its value, and `first` and `last` copy out a `Copy` key. Access order is written as `move_to_last` where a key is read. Growing goes over the old slots in order and fixes the links in a second pass, since following the list missed the cache at every key and made growing 5 times slower; a loop follows the list, which over a table much bigger than the cache waits on memory at each key, as fastutil's does. Checked against Java 25 and fastutil 8.5.18 at once, with the same random operations on all three, over `int`, `long`, string and record keys: Java and fastutil agreed at every step, and Calx with both, at all 375,060 checks of 60 runs and 3 million operations. | Phase 3 |
| D110 | 2026-10-27 | Deque: a ring added to and taken from at both ends | `Deque<T>` is Java's `ArrayDeque` and the `Queue` and `Deque` vanilla keeps in a `LinkedList` or an `ArrayDeque`, whose order is only the order elements went in at each end, so one native type stands for all of them. It's a list's pointer, length and capacity and the index of the first element, and the element `k` after the first is at `head + k`, less the capacity past the end, so the capacity needn't be a power of two and a literal is built as a list's is. Its ends are `push_first`, `push_last`, `pop_first`, `pop_last`, `first` and `last`, the names a `LinkedMap`'s ends have (D109), and a Java or Rust name such as `poll`, `offer` or `push_back` is an error that gives the Calx one; Java's `push` and `pop` are at the front, which the error says. It's indexed from the first element, as Rust's `VecDeque` is and Java's isn't. `==` and `Hash` go over the elements from the first; it has no `Order` and no `JavaHash`, as `ArrayDeque` has neither. Growing doubles, as a list does, and moves the shorter run of a wrapped ring; a loop goes over at most two plain runs. Checked against Java 25 with the same 800,000 random operations, at 84 checkpoints, and clean under AddressSanitizer; over 10 million `i64`s it is 1.2 to 2.4 times as fast as Rust's `VecDeque` at pushing, popping, a short queue, both ends, loops and random reads. | Phase 3 |
| D111 | 2026-10-27 | while let | `while let pattern = value { }` runs while the pattern fits the value, worked out again before each pass, and unwraps an optional as `if let` does, so a queue drains with `while let x = q.pop_first()`, as vanilla's light, fluid and pathfinding code drains its queues. The parser makes it `loop { if let pattern = value { body } else { break } }`, marked as from a `while`, so the checker, the ownership pass and the emitter need nothing new and `break` and `continue` mean what they do in any loop; the mark keeps it a statement, never a block's value, makes `break` with a value an error as in a `while`, and names `while let` in its errors. A pattern that fits every value is an error, as in `if let`, since the loop could stop only at a `break`; Rust only warns. | Phase 3 |
| D112 | 2026-10-27 | SortedMap and SortedSet | `SortedMap<K, V>` and `SortedSet<T>` keep their keys in their `Order`, as Java's `TreeMap` and `TreeSet` and fastutil's tree tables do, so they stand for all of them; a comparator is a key type with that `Order`. They're a B+ tree in a template the emitter includes once for each table type: leaves of about 1 KB with keys and values in separate arrays, linked both ways, so a loop and a range are walks along them, and inner nodes of up to 64 children, searched by counting for numbers, which vectorizes, and by halves without branches for other keys. Keys that own memory are copied into the separators, which can outlive them.
